manifestation: During lessons or art-fair meetings, I’ll catch myself stopping mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase from someone else instead of responding normally, and then I feel embarrassed because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\n- key: S023\n  name: Negative_Symptoms\n  description: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.\n  manifestation: I still care about my students and friends, but for many months it has felt like the warmth and drive have drained out of me, so I let book club, theater planning, and even simple phone calls slide unless someone pushes me.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still teaching, but I’ve needed coworkers to cover parts of project lessons and help me get through planning meetings, and I’ve stopped being reliable for book club, theater, and neighborhood events.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 2 months\n  manifestation: This overall change in how I think, work, and connect with people manifestation: The coded-message worries and the voice I sometimes answer have been happening for about four months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Um... well, it's mostly because of some things at work. I teach at a secondary school, and... I just haven't been able to shake this feeling lately. \n\nIt's about the staff emails, mostly. And the morning announcements. It feels like... well, like they're actually coded messages directed at me. Some of my colleagues have gently tried to show me that they're just standard messages sent to everyone on staff, but... I don't know. I just can't seem to shake the feeling that they're about me.\n\nDoctor: Besides these feelings about the messages, have you been hearing voices or sounds that others around you don't seem to hear?\n\nPatient: I... suppose so. I just... haven't been going.\n\nDoctor: When you first started noticing these things about the messages and hearing the voice four months ago, did it come on suddenly over days or a week or so, or did it develop more gradually over a longer period before you really noticed it?\n\nPatient: I'm... not really sure. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello. Um... well, it's mostly because of some things at work. I teach at a secondary school, and... I just haven't been able to shake this feeling lately. \n\nIt's about the staff emails, mostly. And the morning announcements. It feels like... well, like they're actually coded messages directed at me. Some of my colleagues have gently tried to show me that they're just standard messages sent to everyone on staff, but... I don't know. I just can't seem to shake the feeling that they're about me.\n\nDoctor: Besides these feelings about the messages, have you been hearing voices or sounds that others around you don't seem to hear?\n\nPatient: I... suppose so. Just occasionally. \n\nUm, mostly when it's very quiet. Like after the students leave for the day, or... at home. I'll hear a woman's voice. She just... says my name, usually. Or makes a brief comment about whatever I'm doing. It sounds perfectly clear, so... I usually answer her before I realize that I'm completely alone in the room.\n\nDoctor: Over the past few weeks or months, have you noticed any periods where you needed much less sleep than usual but still felt energetic and productive, or times when your thoughts were racing or you felt unusually energized?\n\nPatient: Um... no. I don't think so. \n\nMy sleep hasn't really changed. And I haven't felt... unusually energized or anything like that. Just... normal, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings that the messages are directed at you and hearing the woman's voice? I didn't really keep track of how it started.\n\nDoctor: Besides the changes you mentioned at work and socially, have you noticed any changes in how you take care of yourself — like your hygiene, or the way you dress, or keeping your living space clean?\n\nPatient: No... I haven't really noticed anything different with that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to around four months ago when this started, was there anything significant happening in your life at that time — like a major stressful event, loss, or significant change — that seemed to come before you noticed these feelings about the messages and hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think so. There wasn't anything like that. Just... normal life, really. Nothing stands out.\n\nDoctor: Over these four months, have the messages you're receiving and the voice you're hearing stayed about the same, or have they gotten noticeably worse or more intense?\n\nPatient: I suppose they've mostly... stayed the same. I haven't really noticed them getting worse or anything. They're just... there. I don't really know how else to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the way you speak or think — like your thoughts feeling jumbled or hard to follow, or people telling you that what you're saying doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I mean, I teach all day, so people seem to understand me just fine. No one has said I don't make sense. My thoughts are just... normal. I haven't noticed any problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things that seemed strange or unusual to you, or have people commented that your behavior seemed odd or didn't make sense — apart from the messages and the voice we've already discussed?\n\nPatient: I... well, sometimes. They just have to be in a certain pattern. And sometimes I... I might just repeat what someone else just said, instead of answering them. I don't really know why I do it. It's a bit embarrassing, really, because it stops the lesson. But... I try not to let it happen too much.\n\nDoctor: When you repeat what someone else just said instead of answering them — does that happen often during conversations, or is it mainly just during those specific moments like the art-fair meetings you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Mostly just... during those times. It just... it interrupts what I'm supposed to be doing. Which is... well, embarrassing. I try to just move on.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking with your colleagues or teaching your students, do you find that your emotions come through naturally — like smiling when something is funny, or showing concern when someone is upset — or do people sometimes comment that you seem emotionally flat or distant?\n\nPatient: I don't think anyone has said I seem flat. I... I still care about my students. And my friends. It's just... \n\nI guess for a long time now, maybe months, I just feel... drained. Like the warmth is gone. I don't really do much anymore. I stopped going to my book club. I used to help plan the local theater stuff, but I let that slide too. Even just... answering the phone. I don't really do it unless someone pushes me. I just... don't have the drive for it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Has it been going on continuously for weeks or months?\n\nPatient: Um... I suppose it's been a while now. \n\nMaybe... about four months? Yes, around four months that I've noticed the messages... and the voice. It's just been going on since then.\n\nDoctor: Over these past four months, have you noticed any changes in how well you're able to do your job as a teacher, or in your relationships with colleagues and friends?\n\nPatient: I'm... still teaching. It's mostly fine. I suppose I have needed... a little help from my coworkers lately. Just covering parts of the project lessons, or... getting through some of our planning meetings. \n\nAnd socially... I guess I haven't been very reliable. I've stopped going to my book club... and the theater, and... neighborhood things. I just... haven't been going.\n\nDoctor: When you first started noticing these things about the messages and hearing the voice four months ago, did it come on suddenly over days or a week or so, or did it develop more gradually over a longer period before you really noticed it?\n\nPatient: I'm... not really sure. It's hard to say, exactly. I just... realized it was happening at some point. I didn't really keep track of how it started.\n\nDoctor: Besides the changes you mentioned at work and socially, have you noticed any changes in how you take care of yourself — like your hygiene, or the way you dress, or keeping your living space clean?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I mean, I still get up and get dressed for work every day. My house is... it's fine. Just the usual. I haven't really noticed anything different with that.\n\nDoctor: Looking back to around four months ago when this started, was there anything significant happening in your life at that time — like a major stressful event, loss, or significant change — that seemed to come before you noticed these feelings about the messages and hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I don't... I don't think so. There wasn't anything like that. Just... normal life, really. I haven't really noticed them getting worse or anything. They're just... there. I don't really know how else to describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in the way you speak or think — like your thoughts feeling jumbled or hard to follow, or people telling you that what you're saying doesn't make sense?\n\nPatient: No... I don't think so. I mean, I teach all day, so people seem to understand me just fine. No one has said I don't make sense. My thoughts are just... normal. I haven't noticed any problem.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself doing things that seemed strange or unusual to you, or have people commented that your behavior seemed odd or didn't make sense — apart from the messages and the voice we've already discussed?\n\nPatient: I... well, sometimes. Just small things. Like during my lessons, or when we have the art-fair meetings... I might stop what I'm doing. Just to... arrange the markers on the desk. Or the papers. They just have to be in a certain pattern. And sometimes I... I might just repeat what someone else just said, instead of answering them. I don't really know why I do it. It's a bit embarrassing, really, because it stops the lesson. But... I try not to let it happen too much.\n\nDoctor: When you repeat what someone else just said instead of answering them — does that happen often during conversations, or is it mainly just during those specific moments like the art-fair meetings you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Mostly just... during those times. The lessons or the meetings. I'll just catch myself doing it... repeating a phrase instead of responding normally. Or stopping mid-task to fix the papers like I said. It doesn't happen all the time. It just... it interrupts what I'm supposed to be doing. Which is... well, embarrassing. I try to just move on.\n\nDoctor: When you're talking with your colleagues or teaching your students, do you find that your emotions come through naturally — like smiling when something is funny, or showing concern when someone is upset — or do people sometimes comment that you seem emotionally flat or distant?\n\nPatient: I don't think anyone has said I seem flat. I... I still care about my students. And my friends. It's just... \n\nI guess for a long time now, maybe months, I just feel... drained. Like the warmth is gone. I don't really do much anymore. I just... don't have the drive for it.\n\nDoctor: When you think back to before these four months started, would you say you were more active and engaged with your hobbies and social life — like going to the book club and theater regularly — or had you already been pulling back from those things gradually over a longer period?\n\nPatient: I suppose... it wasn't just four months ago. The pulling back, I mean. It's been... longer. Since my youngest moved away for college. That was about... a year and two months ago, maybe? That's when everything just started to... shift. I catch myself... rearranging my whiteboard markers, or lining up papers in a... a very specific pattern. Or someone will say something, and I'll just repeat their phrase back to them instead of answering normally. \n\nIt's... it's just very embarrassing. Because it interrupts what I'm actually supposed to be doing. So... yes, I guess that part is the biggest problem right now.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the urge to arrange the markers in that specific pattern or repeat what someone just said — does it feel like something you *have* to do to relieve tension or anxiety, or does it feel more like something happening to you that you can't control, even though you don't want it to happen?\n\nPatient: I don't... it's not like I'm anxious and trying to fix it. It's just... happening. I'll just be in the middle of a lesson, or one of those art-fair meetings, and I'll just... stop. And then everyone is looking at me, and I'm not doing what I'm supposed to be doing. It's just... very embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: When you're arranging the markers or repeating what someone said, do you feel like your own mind is making you do it, or does it feel more like your body is just... doing it on its own, like it's not really coming from you?\n\nPatient: I don't know... it's hard to explain. I just catch myself doing it... moving the markers, or making the papers sit in that exact pattern. I just catch myself doing it... moving the markers, or making the papers sit in that exact pattern. Or I just hear myself repeating the words the other person said instead of answering them. \n\nIt just happens. And it stops me from doing my job... which is embarrassing. I don't really want to talk about it anymore.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you repeat what someone says or arrange the markers, does it feel like it's coming from your own thoughts and intentions, or does it feel more like something is making your body do it against your will?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 56 / secondary_school_teacher / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"During lessons or art-fair meetings, I’ll catch myself stopping mid-task to rearrange markers or papers in a very particular pattern, or I’ll repeat a phrase from someone else instead of responding normally, and then I feel embarrassed because it interrupts what I’m supposed to be doing.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I don't think anything else is making me do it. It's not like that. 

I just... like I said, I'll be in a lesson, and I just stop what I'm doing to put the markers or the papers in that pattern. Or I repeat the phrase. I don't plan it, it just... happens. And it interrupts my work. 

I really don't want to keep going over this. I already told you what happens, and... it's just very embarrassing.

The clinical presentation is most consistent with F20.1 (Paranoid schizophrenia), characterized by the prominence of delusions and hallucinations with relatively preserved functioning compared to disorganized type.
